For (b), assume that $\mathbb { A } ^ { 2 } \setminus ( 0 , 0 )$ is afine, and determine its coordinate ring; then get a contradiction using Corollary 4.5.] 

Part III 

Applications 

	### Chapter 5

	### Projective and birational geometry

The first part of $\ S 5$ aims to generalise the content of §§3–4 to projective varieties; this is fairly mechanical, with just a few essential points. The remainder of the section is concerned with birational geometry, taking up the function field $k ( V )$ from the end of $\ S 4 ;$ this is material which fits equally well into the projective or afine context. 

	#### 5.0 Why projective varieties?

The cubic curve 

$$
C: \left(Y ^ {2} Z = X ^ {3} + a X Z ^ {2} + b Z ^ {3}\right) \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {2}
$$

is the union of two afine curves 

$$
\begin{array}{l} C _ {0}: (y ^ {2} = x ^ {3} + a x + b) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {2} \quad (\text { the   piece } (Z = 1) \text { of } C) \quad \text { and } \\ C _ {1}: (z _ {1} = x _ {1} ^ {3} + a x z _ {1} ^ {2} + b z _ {1} ^ {3}) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {2} \quad (\text { the   piece } (Y = 1)), \end{array}
$$

glued together by the isomorphism 

by 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} C _ {0} \setminus (y = 0) & \longrightarrow & C _ {1} \setminus (z _ {1} = 0) \\ (x, y) & \longmapsto & (x / y, 1 / y). \end{array}
$$

As a much simpler example, $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ with homogeneous coordinates $( X , Y )$ is the union of $2$ copies of $\mathbb { A } ^ { 1 }$ with coordinates $x _ { 0 } , y _ { 1 }$ respectively, glued together by the isomorphism 

by 

$$
\begin{array}{r c l} \mathbb {A} ^ {1} \setminus (x _ {0} = 0) & \longrightarrow & \mathbb {A} ^ {1} \setminus (y _ {1} = 0) \\ x _ {0} & \longmapsto & 1 / x _ {0}. \end{array}
$$

The usual picture is 

![[d06a3d940d17af10c987ce2e5ba422f38a2d9708ecba1aed088f995994876635.jpg]]

Figure 5.1: $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ glued from two $\mathbb { A } ^ { 1 } \mathrm { s }$

(the arrows ↔ denote glueing). 

It’s important to understand that these varieties are strictly bigger than any afine variety. In fact, with the natural notion of morphism (to be introduced shortly), it can be seen that there are no nonconstant morphisms $\mathbb { P } ^ { 1 } \to \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ or $C \to \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ for any n (see Ex. 5.1 and Ex. 5.12, and the discussion in (8.10)). 

One solution to this problem is to define the notion of ‘abstract variety’ V as a union $V = \cup V _ { i }$ of afine varieties, modulo suitable glueing. By analogy with the definition of manifolds in topology, this is an attractive idea, but it leads to many more technical dificulties. Using projective varieties sidesteps these problems by working in the ready-made ambient space $\mathbb P ^ { n }$ , so that (apart from a little messing about with homogeneous polynomials) they are not much harder to study than afine varieties. In fact, although this may not be clear at an elementary level, projective varieties to a quite remarkable extent provide a natural framework for studying varieties (this is briefly discussed from a more advanced point of view in (8.11)). 

	#### 5.1 Graded rings and homogeneous ideals

**Definition** A polynomial $f \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ is homogeneous of degree d $i f$ 

$$
f = \sum a _ {i _ {0} \dots i _ {n}} X _ {0} ^ {i _ {0}} \dots X _ {n} ^ {i _ {n}} w i t h a _ {i _ {0} \dots i _ {n}} \neq 0 o n l y i f i _ {0} + \dots + i _ {n} = d.
$$

Any $f \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ has a unique expression $f = f _ { 0 } + f _ { 1 } + \cdot \cdot \cdot + f _ { N }$ in which $f _ { d }$ is homogeneous of degree d for each $d = 0 , 1 , \dotsc , N$ 

Proposition If f is homogeneous of degree d then 

$$
f (\lambda X _ {0}, \dots , \lambda X _ {n}) = \lambda^ {d} f (X _ {0}, \dots , X _ {n}) \quad f o r a l l \lambda \in k;
$$

if k is an infinite field then the converse also holds. 

**Proof.** Try it and see. 

**Definition** An ideal $I \subset k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ is homogeneous if for all $f \in I ,$ the homogeneous decomposition $f = f _ { 0 } + f _ { 1 } + \cdot \cdot \cdot + f _ { N }$ of f satisfies $f _ { i } \in I$ for all i. 

It is equivalent to say that I is generated by (finitely many) homogeneous polynomials. 

	#### 5.2 The homogeneous V -I correspondences

Let $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ be n-dimensional projective space over a field $k ,$ with $X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n }$ as homogeneous coor dinates. Then $f \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ is not a function on $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n . }$ by definition, $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n } = k ^ { n + 1 } \backslash \{ 0 \} / \sim ,$ where ∼ is the equivalence relation given by $( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ) { \overset { \cdot \cdot } { \sim } } ( \lambda X _ { 0 } , \ldots , \lambda X _ { n } )$ for $\lambda \in k \setminus \{ 0 \} ; f$ is a function on $k ^ { n + 1 }$ . Nevertheless, for $P \in \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ , the condition $f ( P ) = 0$ is well defined provided that $f$ is homogeneous: suppose $P = ( X _ { 0 } : \cdots : X _ { n } ) \quad$ , so that $( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ is a representative in $k ^ { n + 1 } \backslash \{ 0 \}$ of the equivalence class of $P .$ Then since $f ( \lambda X ) = \lambda ^ { d } f ( X )$ , if $f ( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ) = 0$ then also $f ( \lambda X _ { 0 } , \ldots , \lambda X _ { n } ) = 0$ , so that the condition $f ( P ) = 0$ is independent of the choice of representative. With this in mind, define as before correspondences 

$$
\left\{\text { homog.   ideals } J \subset k [ X _ {0}, \dots , X _ {n} ] \right\} \xleftrightarrow {V - I} \left\{\text { subsets } X \subset \mathbb {P} _ {k} ^ {n} \right\}
$$

by 

$$
V (J) = \left\{P \in \mathbb {P} _ {k} ^ {n} \mid f (P) = 0 \forall \text { homogeneous } f \in J \right\}
$$

and 

$$
I (X) = \left\{f \in k [ X _ {0}, \dots , X _ {n} ] \mid f (P) = 0 \text {   for   all   } P \in X \right\}.
$$

As an exercise, check that you understand why $I ( X )$ is a homogeneous ideal. 

The correspondences V and I satisfy the same formal properties as the afine V and I correspondences introduced in $\ S 3$ (for example $V ( J _ { 1 } + J _ { 2 } ) = V ( J _ { 1 } ) \cap V ( J _ { 2 } ) )$ . A subset of the form $V ( I )$ is an algebraic subset of $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ , and as in the afine case, $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ has a Zariski topology in which the closed sets are the algebraic subsets. 

	#### 5.3 Projective Nullstellensatz

As with the afine correspondences, it is purely formal that $I ( V ( J ) ) \supset$ rad J for any ideal $J ,$ and that for an algebraic set, $V ( I ( X ) ) = X$ . There’s just one point where care is needed: the trivial ideal $( 1 ) = k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ (the whole ring) defines the empty set in $k ^ { n + 1 }$ , hence also in $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ , which is as it should be; however, the ideal $( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ defines $\{ 0 \}$ in $k ^ { n + 1 }$ , which also corresponds to the empty set in $\mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ . The ideal $( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ is an awkward (empty-set theoretical) exception to several statements in the theory, and is traditionally known as the ‘irrelevant ideal’. 

The homogeneous version of the Nullstellensatz thus becomes: 

**Theorem** Assume that k is an algebraically closed field. Then 

$$
(i) V (J) = \emptyset \iff \operatorname{rad} J \supset (X _ {0}, \dots , X _ {n});
$$

2. $i f V ( J ) \neq \emptyset$ then $I ( V ( J ) ) = \operatorname { r a d } J .$ 

Corollary I and V determine inverse bijections 

$$
\begin{array}{c c c} \left\{ \begin{array}{c} h o m o g e n e o u s r a d i c a l \\ i d e a l s J \subset k [ x _ {0}, \ldots , x _ {n} ] \\ w i t h J \not \supseteq (x _ {0}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \end{array} \right\} & \longleftrightarrow & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} a l g e b r a i c s u b s e t s \\ X \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {n} \end{array} \right\} \\ \bigcup & & \bigcup \\ \left\{ \begin{array}{c} h o m o g e n e o u s p r i m e \\ i d e a l s J \subset k [ x _ {0}, \ldots , x _ {n} ] \\ w i t h J \not \supseteq (x _ {0}, \ldots , x _ {n}) \end{array} \right\} & \longleftrightarrow & \left\{ \begin{array}{c} i r r e d u c i b l e a l g e b r a i c \\ s u b s e t s X \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {n} \end{array} \right\} \end{array}
$$

**Proof.** Let $\pi \colon { \mathbb { A } } ^ { n + 1 } \backslash \{ 0 \}  { \mathbb { P } } ^ { n }$ be the map defining $\mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ . For a homogeneous ideal $J \subset k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ write (in temporary notation) $V ^ { a } ( J ) \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n + 1 }$ for the afine algebraic set defined by J. Then since J is homogeneous, $V ^ { a } ( J )$ has the property 

$$
\left(\alpha_ {0}, \dots , \alpha_ {n}\right) \in V ^ {a} (J) \iff \left(\lambda \alpha_ {0}, \dots , \lambda \alpha_ {n}\right) \in V ^ {a} (J),
$$

and $V ( J ) = V ^ { a } ( J ) \setminus \{ 0 \} / { \sim } \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ . Hence 

$$
V (J) = \emptyset \iff V ^ {a} (J) \subset \{0 \} \iff \operatorname{rad} J \supset (X _ {0}, \dots , X _ {n}),
$$

where the last implication uses the afine Nullstellensatz. Also, if $V ( J ) \neq \emptyset$ then 

$$
f \in I (V (J)) \iff f \in I (V ^ {a} (J)) \iff f \in \operatorname{rad} J. \quad \text { **Q.E.D.** }
$$

The afine subset $V ^ { a } ( J )$ occurring above is called the afine cone over the projective algebraic subset $V ( J )$ 

	#### 5.4 Rational functions on V

Let $V \subset \mathbb { P } _ { k } ^ { n }$ be an irreducible algebraic set, and $I ( V ) \subset k [ X _ { 0 } , \dotsc , X _ { n } ]$ its ideal; there is no direct way of defining regular functions on V in terms of polynomials: an element $F \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ gives a function on the afine cone over V , but (by case $d = 0$ of Proposition 5.1) this will be constant on equivalence classes only if F is homogeneous of degree 0, that is, a constant. So from the start, I work with rational functions only: 

**Definition** A rational function on V is a (partially defined) function $f \colon V \to k$ given by $f ( P ) =$ $g ( P ) / h ( P )$ , where $g , h \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ are homogeneous polynomials of the same degree d. 

Note here that provided $h ( P ) \neq 0$ , the quotient $g ( P ) / h ( P )$ is well defined, since 

$$
g (\lambda \underline {{X}}) / h (\lambda \underline {{X}}) = \lambda^ {d} g (\underline {{X}}) / \lambda^ {d} h (\underline {{X}}) = g (\underline {{X}}) / h (\underline {{X}}) \quad \text { for } 0 \neq \lambda \in k.
$$

Now obviously $g / h$ and $g ^ { \prime } / h ^ { \prime }$ define the same rational function on $V$ if and only if $h ^ { \prime } g - g ^ { \prime } h \in$ $I ( V )$ , so that the set of all rational functions is the field 

$$
k (V) = \left\{\frac {g}{h} \left| \begin{array}{l} g, h \in k [ X _ {0}, \ldots , X _ {n} ] \text {homogeneous} \\ \text {of the same degree, and} h \notin I (V) \end{array} \right. \right\} / \sim ,
$$

where ∼ is the equivalence relation 

$$
\frac {g}{h} \sim \frac {g ^ {\prime}}{h ^ {\prime}} \iff h ^ {\prime} g - g ^ {\prime} h \in I (V).
$$

$k ( V )$ is the (rational) function field of V . 

The following definitions are just as in the afine case. For $f \in k ( V )$ and $P \in V$ , say that $f$ is regular at P if there exists an expression $f = g / h$ , with $g , h$ homogeneous polynomials of the same degree, such that $h ( P ) \neq 0$ . Write 

$$
\operatorname{dom} f = \left\{P \in V \mid f \text {   is   regular   at   } P \right\}
$$

and 

$$
\mathcal {O} _ {V, P} = \left\{f \in k (V) \mid f \text {   is   regular   at   } P \right\}.
$$

Clearly, dom $f \subset V$ is a dense Zariski open set in V (the Proof is as in (4.8, I), and $\mathcal { O } _ { V , P } \subset k ( V )$ is a subring. 

	#### 5.5 Afine covering of a projective variety

Let $V \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ be an irreducible algebraic set, and suppose for simplicity that $V \not \subset ( X _ { i } = 0 )$ for any i. We know that $\mathbb P ^ { n }$ is covered by $n + 1$ afine pieces $\mathbb { A } _ { ( i ) } ^ { n }$ , with afine (inhomogeneous) coordinates 

$$
X _ {0} ^ {(i)}, \ldots , X _ {i - 1} ^ {(i)}, X _ {i + 1} ^ {(i)}, \ldots , X _ {n} ^ {(i)}, \quad \text { where } \quad X _ {j} ^ {(i)} = X _ {j} / X _ {i} \quad \text { for } j \neq i.
$$

Write $V _ { ( i ) } = V \cap \mathbb { A } _ { ( i ) } ^ { n }$ . Then $V _ { ( i ) } \subset \mathbb { A } _ { ( i ) } ^ { n }$ is clearly an afine algebraic set, because 

$$
V _ {(0)} \ni P = (1, x _ {1} ^ {(0)}, \ldots , x _ {n} ^ {(0)})
$$

$$
f \in I (V)
$$

which is a set of polynomial relations in the coordinates $( x _ { 1 } ^ { ( 0 ) } , \ldots , x _ { n } ^ { ( 0 ) } )$ of $P .$ . For clarity, I have taken $i = 0$ in the argument, and will continue to do so whenever convenient. The reader should remember that the same result applies to any of the other afine pieces $V _ { ( i ) }$ . The $V _ { ( i ) }$ are called standard afine pieces of $V$ 

Proposition (i) The correspondence $V \mapsto V _ { ( 0 ) } = V \cap { \mathbb { A } } _ { ( 0 ) } ^ { n }$ gives a bijection 

$$
\left\{ \begin{array}{l} i r r e d u c i b l e a l g. \\ s u b s e t s V \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {n} \end{array} \bigg | V \not \subset (X _ {0} = 0) \right\} \longleftrightarrow \left\{ \begin{array}{l} i r r e d u c i b l e a l g. \\ s u b s e t s V _ {0} \subset \mathbb {A} _ {(0)} ^ {n} \end{array} \right\};
$$

the inverse correspondence is given by taking the closure in the Zariski topology. 

2. Write $I ^ { h } ( V ) \subset k [ X _ { 0 } , \dots , X _ { n } ]$ for the homogeneous ideal of $V \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ introduced in this section and $I ^ { a } ( V _ { ( 0 ) } ) \subset k [ X _ { 1 } , . . . , X _ { n } ]$ for the usual (as in §3) inhomogeneous ideal of $V _ { ( 0 ) } \subset \mathbb { A } _ { ( 0 ) } ^ { n } $ then $I ^ { h } ( V )$ and $I ^ { a } ( V _ { ( 0 ) } )$ are related as follows: 

$$
I ^ {a} = \left\{f (1, X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}) \mid f \in I ^ {h} (V) \right\},
$$

and 

$$
I ^ {h} (V) _ {d} = \left\{X _ {0} ^ {d} f \left(\frac {X _ {1}}{X _ {0}}, \dots , \frac {X _ {n}}{X _ {0}}\right) \mid f \in I ^ {a} (V _ {(0)}), w i t h \deg f \leq d \right\},
$$

where the subscript in $I ^ { h } ( V ) _ { d }$ denotes the piece of degree d. 

3. $k ( V ) \cong k ( V _ { ( 0 ) } )$ , and for $f \in k ( V )$ , the domain of f as a function on $V _ { ( 0 ) }$ is $V _ { ( 0 ) } \cap$ dom $f .$ 

**Proof.** (i) and (ii) are easy. (iii) ${ \mathrm { I f ~ } } g , h \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ are homogeneous of degree $d ,$ and $h \not \in I ( V )$ then $g / h \in k ( V )$ restricted to $V _ { ( 0 ) }$ is the function 

$$
\frac {g (1 , X _ {1} / X _ {0} , \ldots , X _ {n} / X _ {0})}{h (1 , X _ {1} / X _ {0} , \ldots , X _ {n} / X _ {0})};
$$

this defines a map $k ( V ) \to k ( V _ { ( 0 ) } )$ , and it’s easy to see what its inverse is. 

	#### 5.6 Rational maps and morphisms

Rational maps between projective (or afine) varieties are defined using $k ( V )$ : if $V \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ is an irreducible algebraic set, a rational map $V \ \to \ \mathbb { A } ^ { m }$ is a (partially defined) map given by $P \mapsto$ $( f _ { 1 } ( P ) , \ldots , f _ { m } ( P ) )$ , where $f _ { 1 } , \ldots , f _ { m } \in k ( V )$ . A rational map $V \to \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ is defined by $P \mapsto ( f _ { 0 } ( P )$ $f _ { 1 } ( P ) : \cdots : f _ { m } ( P ) )$ where $f _ { 0 } , f _ { 1 } , \ldots , f _ { m } \in k ( V )$ . Notice that if $g \in k ( V )$ is a nonzero element, then $g f _ { 0 } , g f _ { 1 } , \dots , g f _ { m }$ defines the same rational map. Therefore (assuming that V does not map into the smaller projective space $( X _ { 0 } = 0 ) )$ , it would be possible to assume throughout that $f _ { 0 } = 1$ 

Clearly then, there is a bijection between the two sets 

$$
\left\{\text { rational   maps } f \colon V \dashrightarrow \mathbb {A} ^ {m} \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {m} \right\}
$$

and 

$$
\left\{\text { rational   maps } f \colon V \dashrightarrow \mathbb {P} ^ {m} \mid f (V) \not \subset (X _ {0} = 0) \right\},
$$

since either kind of maps is given by m elements $f _ { i } \in k ( V )$ 

**Definition** A rational map $f \colon V \ { \mathrm { ~ -- } } \ \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ is regular at $P \in V$ if there exists an expression $f =$ $\left( f _ { 0 } , f _ { 1 } , \ldots , f _ { m } \right)$ such that 

1. each of $f _ { 0 } , \ldots , f _ { m }$ is regular at $P ;$ and 

2. at least one $f _ { i } ( P ) \neq 0$ 

The second condition is required here in order that the ratio between the $f _ { i }$ is defined at $P .$ If $f$ is regular at $P$ (as before, this is also expressed $P \in$ dom $f )$ then $f \colon U \to \mathbb { A } _ { ( i ) } ^ { m } \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ is a morphism for a suitable open neighbourhood $P \in U \subset V { : }$ just take $\begin{array} { r } { U = \bigcap _ { j } \operatorname { d o m } ( f _ { j } / f _ { i } ) } \end{array}$ where $f _ { i } ( P ) \neq 0 ;$ then f is the morphism given by $\left\{ f _ { j } / f _ { i } \right\} _ { j = 0 , 1 , \ldots , m } .$ 

If $U \subset V$ is an open subset of a projective variety V then a morphism $f \colon U \to W$ is a rational map $f \colon V \ { \mathrm { ~ -- } } \ W$ such that dom $f \supset U$ . So a morphism is just a rational map that is everywhere regular on U. 

	#### 5.7 Examples

1. Rational normal curve. This is a very easy example of an isomorphic embedding $f \colon  { \mathbb { P } } ^ { 1 } \ \stackrel { \cong } { \to } $ $C \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ which generalises the parametrised conic of (1.7), and which occurs throughout projective and algebraic geometry. Define 

$$
f \colon \mathbb {P} ^ {1} \to \mathbb {P} ^ {m} \quad \text { by } \quad (U: V) \mapsto (U ^ {m}: U ^ {m - 1} V: \dots : V ^ {m})
$$

(writing down all monomials of degree m in $U , V )$ . Arguing step by step: 

1. f is a rational map, since it’s given by 

$$
((U / V) ^ {m}, (U / V) ^ {m - 1}, \dots , 1);
$$

2. $f$ is a morphism wherever $V \neq 0$ by the formula just written, and if $V = 0$ then $U \neq 0 .$ so a similar trick with $V / U$ works; 

3. the image of $f$ is the set of points $( X _ { 0 } : \cdot \cdot \cdot : X _ { m } ) \in \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ such that 

$$
(X _ {0}: X _ {1}) = (X _ {1}: X _ {2}) = \dots = (X _ {m - 1}: X _ {m}),
$$

that is, 

$$
X _ {0} X _ {2} = X _ {1} ^ {2}, \quad X _ {0} X _ {3} = X _ {1} X _ {2}, \quad X _ {0} X _ {4} = X _ {1} X _ {3}, \quad \mathrm{etc.}
$$

The equations can be written all together in the extremely convenient determinantal form 

$$
\operatorname{rank} \left( \begin{array}{c c c c c} X _ {0} & X _ {1} & X _ {2} & \ldots & X _ {m - 1} \\ X _ {1} & X _ {2} & X _ {3} & \ldots & X _ {m} \end{array} \right) \leq 1
$$

(the rank condition means exactly that all $2 \times 2$ minors vanish). These are homogeneous equations defining an algebraic set $C \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ ; 

4. the inverse morphism $g : C \to \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ is not hard to find: just take a point of $C$ into the common ratio $( X _ { 0 } : X _ { 1 } ) = \dots = ( X _ { m - 1 } : X _ { m } ) \in \mathbb { P } ^ { 1 }$ . As an exercise, find out for yourself what has to be checked, then check it all. 

2. Linear projection, parametrising a quadric. The map $\pi \colon { \mathbb { P } } ^ { 3 } \ldots { } \mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ given by $( X _ { 0 } , X _ { 1 } , X _ { 2 } , X _ { 3 } ) \mapsto$ $( X _ { 1 } , X _ { 2 } , X _ { 3 } )$ is a rational map, and a morphism outside the point $P _ { 0 } = ( 1 , 0 , 0 , 0 )$ . Let $Q \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ be a quadric hypersurface with $P \in Q$ . Then every point $P$ of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ corresponds to a line $L$ of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ through $P ,$ and L should in general meet $Q$ at $P _ { 0 }$ and a second point $\varphi ( P )$ : for example, if $Q : ( X _ { 0 } X _ { 3 } = X _ { 1 } X _ { 2 } )$ , then $\pi | Q : Q \to \mathbb { P } ^ { 2 }$ has the inverse map 

$$
\varphi \colon \mathbb {P} ^ {2} \dashrightarrow Q \text {given by} (X _ {1}, X _ {2}, X _ {3}) \mapsto (X _ {1} X _ {2} / X _ {3}, X _ {1}, X _ {2}, X _ {3}).
$$

This is essentially the same idea as the parametrisation of the circle in (1.1). 

It is a rewarding exercise (see Ex. 5.2) to find dom π and dom $\varphi _ { ; }$ , and to give a geometric interpretation of the singularities of π and $\varphi .$

Figure 5.2: Projection of quadric surface

	#### 5.8 Birational maps

**Definition** Let V and W be (afine or projective) varieties; then a rational map $f \colon V \ { \mathrm { ~ -- } } \ W$ is birational (or is a birational equivalence) if it has a rational inverse, that is, if there exists a rational map $g \colon W \ {  } \ V$ such that $f \circ g = \operatorname { i d } _ { W }$ and $g \circ f = \operatorname { i d } _ { V }$ 

Proposition The following three conditions on a rational map $f \colon V  W$ are equivalent: 

1. f is a birational equivalence; 

2. f is dominant (see $( 4 . 1 0 ) )$ , and $f ^ { * } \colon k ( W ) \to k ( V )$ is an isomorphism; 

3. there exist open sets $V _ { 0 } ~ \subset ~ V$ and $W _ { 0 } \subset W$ such that $f$ restricted to $V _ { 0 }$ is an isomorphism $f \colon V _ { 0 }  W _ { 0 }$ 

Proof $f ^ { * }$ is defined in the same way as for afine varieties, and $( \mathrm { i } ) \iff ( \mathrm { i i } )$ is as in (4.11). $( \mathrm { i i i } ) \longrightarrow ( \mathrm { i } )$ is clear, since an isomorphism $f \colon V _ { 0 }  W _ { 0 }$ and its inverse $g = f ^ { - 1 } \colon W _ { 0 } \to V _ { 0 }$ are by definition rational maps between V and W . 

The essential implication $( \mathrm { i } ) \Longrightarrow ( \mathrm { i i i } )$ is tricky, although content-free (GOTO (5.9) if you want to avoid a headache): by assumption (i), there are inverse rational maps $f \colon V \to - \to$ W and $g \colon W \ \to - \to$ $V ;$ now set $V ^ { \prime } =$ dom $f \subset V$ and $\varphi = f | V ^ { \prime } \colon V ^ { \prime } \to W$ , and similarly $W ^ { \prime } = \operatorname { d o m } g \subset W$ and $\psi = g | W ^ { \prime } \colon W ^ { \prime } \to V$ . In the diagram 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \psi^ {- 1} V ^ {\prime} \xrightarrow {\psi} V ^ {\prime} \xrightarrow {\varphi} W \\ \bigcap \\ W \end{array}
$$

all the arrows are morphisms, and $\mathrm { i d } _ { W } | \psi ^ { - 1 } V ^ { \prime } = \varphi \circ \psi$ (as morphisms) follows from id<sub>W</sub> $= f \circ g$ (as rational maps). Hence 

$$
\varphi (\psi (P)) = P \quad \text { for   all } P \in \psi^ {- 1} V ^ {\prime}.
$$

Now set $V _ { 0 } = \varphi ^ { - 1 } \psi ^ { - 1 } V ^ { \prime }$ , and $W _ { 0 } = \psi ^ { - 1 } \varphi ^ { - 1 } W ^ { \prime } $ ; then $\varphi \colon V _ { 0 }  \psi ^ { - 1 } V ^ { \prime }$ is a morphism by construction. However, $\psi ^ { - 1 } V ^ { \prime } \subset W _ { 0 }$ , since $P \in \psi ^ { - 1 } V ^ { \prime }$ implies that $\varphi ( \psi ( P ) ) = P$ , so that $P \in$ $\psi ^ { - 1 } \varphi ^ { - 1 } W ^ { \prime } = W _ { 0 }$ . Therefore, ϕ : $V _ { 0 }  W _ { 0 }$ is a morphism, and similarly $\psi \colon W _ { 0 } \to V _ { 0 }$ . **Q.E.D.** 

	#### 5.9 Rational varieties

The notion of birational equivalence discussed in (5.8) is of key importance in algebraic geometry. Condition (iii) in the proposition says that the ‘meat’ of the varieties V and W is the same, although they may difer a bit around the edges; an example of the use of birational transformations is blowing up a singular variety to obtain a nonsingular one, see (6.12) below. An important particular case of Proposition 5.8 is the following result. 

Corollary Given a variety V , the following two conditions are equivalent: 

- the function field $k ( V )$ is a purely transcendental extension of k, that is $k ( V ) \cong k ( t _ { 1 } , \dots , t _ { n } )$ for some n; 

- there exists a dense open set $V _ { 0 } \subset V$ which is isomorphic to a dense open subset $U _ { 0 } \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ 

A variety satisfying these conditions is said to be rational. Condition (b) is a precise version of the statement that $\bar { V }$ can be parametrised by n independent variables. This notion has already appeared implicitly several times in these notes (for example, (1.1), (2.1), (3.11, b), (5.7, II)). A large proportion of the elementary applications of algebraic geometry to other branches of math are related one way or another to rational varieties. 

	#### 5.10 Reduction to a hypersurface

An easy consequence of the discussion of Noether normalisation at the end of $\ S 3$ is that every variety is birational to a hypersurface: firstly, since birational questions only depend on a dense open set, and any open set contains a dense open subset isomorphic to an afine variety (by (4.13)), I only need to consider an afine variety $V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ . It was proved in (3.18) that there exist elements $y _ { 1 } , \ldots , y _ { m + 1 } \in k [ V ]$ which generate the field extension $k \subset k ( V )$ , and such that $y _ { 1 } , \ldots , y _ { m }$ are algebraically independent, and $y _ { m + 1 }$ is algebraic over $k ( y _ { 1 } , \dots , y _ { m } )$ . These elements thus define a morphism $V \to \mathbb { A } ^ { m + 1 }$ which is a birational equivalence of V with a hypersurface $V ^ { \prime } \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { m + 1 }$ 

	#### 5.11 Products

If V and W are two afine varieties then there is a natural sense in which $V \times W$ is again a variety: if $V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ and $W \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { m }$ then $V \times W$ is the subset of $\mathbb { A } ^ { n + m }$ given by 

$$
\left\{\left((\alpha_ {1}, \ldots , \alpha_ {n}); (\beta_ {1}, \ldots , \beta_ {m})\right) \Bigg | \begin{array}{l} f (\underline {{\alpha}}) = 0 \text {   for   all   } f \in I (V) \\ g (\underline {{\beta}}) = 0 \text {   for   all   } g \in I (W) \end{array} \right\}
$$

It’s easy to check that $V \times W$ remains irreducible. Note however that the Zariski topology of the product is not the product of the Zariski topologies (see Ex. 5.10). 

The case of projective varieties is not so obvious; to be able to define products, we need to know that $\mathbb { P } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ is itself a projective variety. Notice that it is definitely not isomorphic to $\mathbb { P } ^ { n + m }$ (see Ex. 5.2, ii). To do this, I use a construction rather similar in spirit to that of (5.7, I): make an embedding (the ‘Segre embedding’) 

$$
\varphi \colon \mathbb {P} ^ {n} \times \mathbb {P} ^ {m} \to S _ {n, m} \subset \mathbb {P} ^ {N},
$$

where $N = ( n + 1 ) ( m + 1 ) - 1$ as follows: $\mathbb P ^ { N }$ is the projective space with homogeneous coordinates 

$$
\left(U _ {i j}\right) _ {\stackrel {i = 0, \ldots , n} {j = 0, \ldots , m}}.
$$

It’s useful to think of the $U _ { i j }$ as being set out in a matrix 

$$
\left( \begin{array}{c c c} U _ {0 0} & \ldots & U _ {0 m} \\ U _ {1 0} & \ldots & \ldots \\ \ldots & \ldots & U _ {n m} \end{array} \right)
$$

Then define $\varphi$ by $( ( X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ) , ( Y _ { 0 } , \ldots , Y _ { m } ) ) \mapsto ( X _ { i } Y _ { j } ) _ { \underset { j = 0 , \ldots , m } { i = 0 , \ldots , n } }$ . This is obviously a well defined morphism, and the image $S _ { n , m }$ is easily seen to be the projective subvariety given by 

$$
\operatorname{rank} \left( \begin{array}{c c c} U _ {0 0} & \ldots & U _ {0 m} \\ U _ {1 0} & \ldots & \ldots \\ \ldots & \ldots & U _ {n m} \end{array} \right) \leq 1, \quad \text {   that   is,   } \det \left| \begin{array}{c c} U _ {i k} & U _ {i \ell} \\ U _ {j k} & U _ {j \ell} \end{array} \right| = 0
$$

for all $i , j = 0 , \ldots , n$ and $k , \ell = 0 , \ldots , m .$ 

We get an inverse map $S _ { n , m } \to \mathbb { P } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ as follows. For $P \in S _ { n , m }$ there exists at least one pair $( i , j )$ such that $U _ { i j } ( P ) \neq 0 ;$ fixing this $( i , j )$ , send 

$$
S _ {n, m} \ni P \mapsto ((U _ {0 j}, \dots , U _ {n j}), (U _ {i 0}, \dots , U _ {i m})) \in \mathbb {P} ^ {n} \times \mathbb {P} ^ {m}.
$$

Note that the choice of $( i , j )$ doesn’t matter, since the matrix $U _ { i j } ( P )$ has rank 1, and hence all its rows and all its columns are proportional. 

From this it is not hard to see that if $V \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ and $W \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { m }$ are projective varieties, then $V \times W \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n } \times \mathbb { P } ^ { m } \cong S _ { n , m } \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { N }$ is again a projective variety (see Ex. 5.11).

$$

in $k [ x _ { 1 } , z _ { 1 } ]$ , with $Q _ { m }$ and $R _ { m - 1 }$ homogeneous of the indicated degrees.

$$

if m is even, and an analogous expression if m is odd.

Chapter 6 

	### Chapter 6

	### Tangent space and nonsingularity, dimension

	#### 6.1 Nonsingular points of a hypersurface

Suppose $f \in k [ X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ is irreducible, $f \not \in k ,$ and set $V = V ( f ) \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ ; let $P = ( a _ { 1 } , \ldots , a _ { n } ) \in V$ and ` be a line through P . Since $P \in V$ , obviously $P$ is a root of $f | \ell$ 

Question: When is P a multiple root of $f | \ell ?$ 

Answer: If and only if ` is contained in the afine linear subspace 

$$
T _ {P} V: \left(\sum_ {i} \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} (P) \cdot (X _ {i} - a _ {i}) = 0\right) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {n},
$$

called the tangent space to $V$ at $P .$ . 

![[6e48c8b1940d8012880794f9e109c4c7c73293507a04db3afb2034f83b1101da.jpg]]

Figure 6.1: Tangent space

To prove this, parametrise ` as 

$$
\ell : X _ {i} = a _ {i} + b _ {i} T,
$$

where $P = ( a _ { 1 } , \ldots , a _ { n } ) $ and $\left( b _ { 1 } , \ldots , b _ { n } \right)$ is the slope or direction vector of `. Then $f | \ell = f ( , \dots , a _ { i } +$ $b _ { i } T , \dots , ) = g ( T )$ is a polynomial in $T ,$ , and we know that $( T = 0 )$ is one root of $g .$ . Hence 

0 is a multiple root of $g \iff \frac { \partial g } { \partial T } ( 0 ) = 0 ,$ 

that is, 

$$
\Longleftrightarrow \sum_ {i} b _ {i} \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} (P) = 0 \Longleftrightarrow \ell \subset T _ {P} V.
$$

**Definition** $P \in V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ is a nonsingular point of V if $\partial f / \partial X _ { i } ( P ) \neq 0$ for some $i ;$ otherwise $P$ is a singular point, or a singularity of $V .$ 

Obviously $T _ { P } V$ is an $( n { - } 1 )$ -dimensional afine subspace of $\mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ if $P$ is nonsingular, and $T _ { P } V = \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ if $P \in V$ is singular. 

	#### 6.2 Remarks

- The derivatives $\partial f / \partial X _ { i } ( P )$ appearing above are formal algebraic operations (that is, $\partial / \partial X _ { i }$ takes $X _ { i } ^ { n }$ into $n X _ { i } ^ { n - 1 } )$ ; no calculus is involved. 

- Suppose $k = \mathbb { R }$ or <sup>C</sup>, and that $\partial f / \partial X _ { i } ( P ) \neq 0 ;$ for clarity let me take $i \ : = \ : 1$ . Then the map $p \colon \mathbb { A } ^ { n } \to \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ defined by $( X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { n } ) \mapsto ( f , X _ { 2 } , \dots , X _ { n } )$ has nonvanishing Jacobian determinant at $P ,$ so that by the inverse function theorem, there exists a neighbourhood $P \in U \subset { \mathbb { A } } ^ { n }$ such that $p | U \colon U \to p ( U ) \subset { \mathbb { A } } ^ { n }$ is a difeomorphism of the neighbourhood U with an open set $p ( U )$ of $\mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ (in the usual topology of $\mathbb { R } ^ { n } { \mathrm { ~ o r ~ } } \mathbb { C } ^ { n } )$ ; that $\mathrm { i s } , p | U$ is bijective, and both p and $p ^ { - 1 }$ are diferentiable functions of real or complex variables. In other words, $( f , X _ { 2 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ form a new diferentiable coordinate system on $\mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ near $P ;$ this implies that a neighbourhood of $P$ in $V : ( f = 0 )$ is difeomorphic to an open set in $\mathbb { A } ^ { n - 1 }$ with coordinates $( X _ { 2 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ . Thus near a nonsingular point $P _ { \mathrm { : } }$ , V is a manifold with $( X _ { 2 } , \ldots , X _ { n } )$ as local parameters. 

**Proposition 6.3** $V _ { \mathrm { n o n s i n g } } = \left\{ P \in V \mid P \right.$ is nonsingular	 is a dense open set of V for the Zariski topology. 

**Proof.** The complement of $V _ { \mathrm { n o n s i n g } }$ is the set $V _ { \mathrm { s i n g } }$ of singular points, which is defined by $\partial f / \partial X _ { i } ( P ) =$ 0 for all $i ,$ that is 

$$
V _ {\mathrm{sing}} = V \Big (f, \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {1}}, \ldots , \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {n}} \Big) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {n},
$$

which is closed by definition of the Zariski topology. Since V is irreducible (by (3.11, a), to show that the open $V _ { \mathrm { n o n s i n g } }$ is dense, I only have to show it’s nonempty (by Proposition 4.2); arguing by contradiction, suppose that it’s empty, that is, suppose $V = V ( f ) = V _ { \mathrm { s i n g } }$ . Then each of the polynomials $\partial f / \partial X _ { i }$ must vanish on $V _ { ; }$ , therefore (by (3.11) once more) they must be divisible by $f$ in $k [ X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { n } ] ;$ but viewed as a polynomial in $X _ { i } , \partial f / \partial X _ { i }$ has degree strictly smaller than $f ,$ so that f divides $\partial f / \partial X _ { i }$ implies that in fact $\partial f / \partial X _ { i } = 0$ as a polynomial. Over $\mathbb { C }$ , this is obviously only possible if $X _ { i }$ does not appear in $f ,$ and if this happens for all i then $f = \mathrm { c o n s t . } \in \mathbb { C } .$ , which is excluded. Over a general field $k , \partial f / \partial X _ { i } = 0$ is only possible if $f$ is an inseparable polynomial in 

$X _ { i } ,$ , that ${ \mathrm { i s } } ,$ char $k = p ,$ and $X _ { i }$ only appears in $f$ as the pth power $X _ { i } ^ { p }$ . If this happens for each $i ,$ then by the argument given in (3.16), f is a pth power in $k [ X _ { 1 } , \dots , X _ { n } ]$ ; this contradicts the fact that f is irreducible. **Q.E.D.** 

	#### 6.4 Tangent space

**Definition** Let $V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ be a subvariety, with $V \ni P = ( a _ { 1 } , . . . , a _ { n } )$ . For any $f \in k [ X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ write 

$$
f _ {P} ^ {(1)} = \sum_ {i} \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} (P) \cdot (X _ {i} - a _ {i}).
$$

This is an afine linear polynomial (that is, linear plus constant), the ‘first order part’ of f at $P .$ Now define the tangent space to $V$ at P by 

$$
T _ {P} V = \bigcap \left(f _ {P} ^ {(1)} = 0\right) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {n},
$$

where the intersection takes place over all $f \in I ( V )$ 

**Proposition 6.5** The function $V  \mathbb { N }$ defined by $P \mapsto$ dim $T _ { P } V$ is an upper semicontinuous function (in the Zariski topology of V ). In other words, for any integer r, the subset 

$$
S (r) = \left\{P \in V \mid \dim T _ {P} V \geq r \right\} \subset V
$$

is closed. 

**Proof.** Let $\left( f _ { 1 } , \ldots , f _ { m } \right)$ be a set of generators of $I ( V )$ ; it is easy to see that for any $g \in I ( V )$ the linear part $g _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ of $g$ is a linear combination of those of the $f _ { i } ,$ , so that the definition of $T _ { P } V$ simplifies to 

$$
T _ {P} V = \bigcap_ {i = 1} ^ {m} \left(f _ {i, P} ^ {(1)} = 0\right) \subset \mathbb {A} ^ {n}.
$$

Then by elementary linear algebra, 

$$
\begin{array}{l}P\in S(r)\iff \text{the matrix}\left(\frac{\partial f}{\partial X_{i}} (P)\right)_{\substack{i = 1,\ldots ,m\\ j = 1,\ldots ,n}}\text{has rank}\leq n - r\\ \iff \text{every} (n - r + 1)\times (n - r + 1)\text{minor vanishes.} \end{array}
$$

Now each entry $\partial f _ { i } / \partial X _ { j } ( P )$ of the matrix is a polynomial function of $P ;$ thus each minor is a determinant of a matrix of polynomials, and so is itself a polynomial. Hence $S ( r ) \subset V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ is an algebraic subset. **Q.E.D.** 

**Corollary-Definition** 6.6 There exists an integer r and a dense open subset $V _ { 0 } \subset V$ such that 

$$
\dim T _ {P} V = r \text {   for   } P \in V _ {0}, \text {   and   } \dim T _ {P} V \geq r \text {   for   all   } P \in V.
$$

Define r to be the dimension of V , and write dim $V = r ;$ and say that $P \in V$ is nonsingular $i f$ dim $T _ { P } V = r _ { \mathrm { { i } } }$ , and singular if dim $T _ { P } V > r$ . A variety V is nonsingular if it is nonsingular at each point $P \in V$ 

**Proof.** Let $r = \operatorname* { m i n } \{ \dim T _ { P } V \}$ , taken over all points $P \in V$ . Then clearly 

$$
S (r - 1) = \emptyset , \quad S (r) = V, \quad \text { and } \quad S (r + 1) \subsetneq V;
$$

therefore $S ( r ) \setminus S ( r + 1 ) = \left\{ P \in V \big | \dim T _ { P } V = r \right\}$ is open and nonempty. **Q.E.D.** 

	#### 6.7 dim V = tr deg k(V ) – the hypersurface case

It follows from Proposition 6.3 that if $V = V ( f ) \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ is a hypersurface defined by some nonconstant polynomial $f ,$ , then dim $V = n - 1$ . On the other hand, for a hypersurface, $k [ V ] = k [ X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ] / ( f )$ so that, assuming that $f$ involves $X _ { 1 }$ in a nontrivial way, the function field of V is of the form 

$$
k (V) = k (X _ {2}, \dots , X _ {n}) [ X _ {1} ] / (f),
$$

that is, it is built up from k by adjoining $n - 1$ algebraically independent elements, then making a primitive algebraic extension. 

**Definition** If $k \subset K$ is a field extension, the transcendence degree of K over k is the maximum number of elements of K algebraically independent over k. It is denoted $\operatorname { t r } \mathrm { d e g } _ { k } K$ 

The elementary theory of transcendence degree of a field extension $K / k$ is formally quite similar to that of the dimension of a vector space: given $\alpha _ { 1 } , \ldots , \alpha _ { m } \in K$ , we know what it means for them to be algebraically independent over $k \ \left( \mathrm { s e e \ ( 3 . 1 3 ) } \right)$ ; they span the transcendental part of the extension if $K / k ( \alpha _ { 1 } , \ldots , \alpha _ { m } )$ is algebraic; and they form a transcendence basis if they are algebraically independent and span. Then it is an easy theorem that a transcendence basis is a maximal algebraically independent set, and a minimal spanning set, and that any two transcendence bases of $K / k$ have the same number of elements (see Ex. 6.1). 

Thus for a hypersurface $V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ , dim $V = n - 1 = \operatorname { t r } \deg _ { k } k ( V )$ . The rest of this section is concerned with proving that the equality dim $V = \operatorname { t r } \deg _ { k } k ( V )$ holds for all varieties, by reducing to the case of a hypersurface. The first thing to show is that for a point $P \in V$ of a variety, the tangent space $T _ { P } V$ , which so far has been discussed in terms of a particular coordinate system in the ambient space $\mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ , is in fact an intrinsic property of a neighbourhood of $P \in V$ 

	#### 6.8 Intrinsic nature of $T _ { P } V$

From now on, given $P = ( a _ { 1 } , \dots , a _ { n } ) \in V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ , I take new coordinates $X _ { i } ^ { \prime } = X _ { i } - a _ { i }$ to bring $P$ to the origin, and thus assume that $P = ( 0 , \ldots , 0 )$ . Then $T _ { P } V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ is a vector subspace of $k ^ { n }$ . 

Notation Write $m _ { P } = \mathrm { i d e a l }$ of $P$ in $k [ V ]$ , and 

$$
M _ {P} = \text { the   ideal } (X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}) \subset k [ X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n} ].
$$

Then of course $m _ { P } = M _ { P } / I ( V ) \subset k [ V ]$ 

**Theorem** In the above notation, 

- there is a natural isomorphism of vector spaces 

$$
(T _ {P} V) ^ {*} = m _ {P} / m _ {P} ^ {2},
$$

where $( ) ^ { * }$ denotes the dual of a vector space. 

- $I f f \in k [ V ]$ is such that $f ( P ) \neq 0$ , and $V _ { f } \subset V$ is the standard afine open as in $\left( 4 . 1 3 \right)$ , then the natural map 

$$
T _ {P} (V _ {f}) \rightarrow T _ {P} V
$$

is an isomorphism. 

**Proof.** of (a) Write $( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * }$ for the vector space of linear forms on $k ^ { n }$ ; this is the vector space with basis $X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n }$ . Since $P = ( 0 , \ldots , 0 )$ , for any $f \in k [ X _ { 1 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ , the linear part $f _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ is naturally an element of $( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * }$ ; define a map d: $M _ { P } \to ( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * }$ by taking $f \in M _ { P }$ into $d f = f _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) }$ 

Now d is surjective, since the $X _ { i } \in M _ { P }$ go into the natural basis of $( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * } ;$ also ker $d = M _ { P } ^ { 2 }$ , since 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} f _ {P} ^ {(1)} = 0 \iff f \text {   starts   with   quadratic   terms   in   } X _ {1}, \ldots , X _ {n} \\ \iff f \in M _ {P} ^ {2}. \end{array}
$$

Hence $M _ { P } / M _ { P } ^ { 2 } \cong ( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * }$ . This is statement (a) for the special case $V = \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ . In the general case, dual to the inclusion $T _ { P } V \subset k ^ { n }$ , there is a restriction map $( k ^ { n } ) ^ { * } \to ( T _ { P } V ) ^ { * }$ , taking a linear form λ on $k ^ { n }$ into its restriction to $T _ { P } V ;$ composing then defines a map 

$$
D: M _ {P} \to (k ^ {n}) ^ {*} \to (T _ {P} V) ^ {*}.
$$

The composite $D$ is surjective since each factor is. I claim that the kernel of D is just $M _ { P } ^ { 2 } + I ( V )$ ， so that 

$$
m _ {P} / m _ {P} ^ {2} = M _ {P} / (M _ {P} ^ {2} + I (V)) \cong (T _ {P} V) ^ {*},
$$

as required. To prove the claim, 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} {f \in \ker D \iff f _ {P} ^ {(1)} | T _ {P} V = 0} \\ {\iff f _ {P} ^ {(1)} = \sum_ {i} a _ {i} g _ {i, P} ^ {(1)} \mathrm{forsome} g _ {i} \in I (V)} \end{array}
$$

(since $T _ { P } V \subset k ^ { n }$ is the vector subspace defined by $( g _ { P } ^ { ( 1 ) } = 0 )$ for $g \in I ( V ) )$ ) 

$$
\Longleftrightarrow f - \sum_ {i} a _ {i} g _ {i} \in M _ {P} ^ {2} \text {   for   some   } g _ {i} \in I (V) \Longleftrightarrow f \in M _ {P} ^ {2} + I (V).
$$

The Proof of (b) of Theorem 6.8 is left to the reader (see Ex. 6.2). **Q.E.D.** 

**Corollary 6.9** $T _ { P } V$ only depends on a neighbourhood of $P \in V$ up to isomorphism. More precisely, if $P \in V _ { 0 } \subset V$ and $Q \in W _ { 0 } \subset W$ are open subsets of afine varieties, and $\varphi \colon V _ { 0 }  W _ { 0 }$ an isomorphism taking $P$ into $Q ,$ there is a natural isomorphism $T _ { P } V _ { 0 }  T _ { Q } W _ { 0 } ;$ hence dim $T _ { P } V _ { 0 } =$ dim $T _ { Q } W _ { 0 }$ 

In particular, $i f V$ and $W$ are birationally equivalent varieties then dim $V = \dim W$ 

**Proof.** By passing to a smaller neighbourhood of P in V , I can assume $V _ { 0 }$ is isomorphic to an afine variety (Proposition 4.13). Then so is $W _ { 0 } .$ and $\varphi$ induces an isomorphism $k [ V _ { 0 } ] \cong k [ W _ { 0 } ]$ taking m into $m _ { Q }$ . The final sentence holds because by (5.8), V and $W$ contain dense open subsets which are isomorphic. 

**Theorem 6.10** For any variety $V ,$ dim $V = \operatorname { t r } \deg { k ( V ) }$ 

**Proof.** This is known if V is a hypersurface. On the other hand, every variety is birational to a hypersurface (by (5.10)), and both sides of the required relation are the same for birationally equivalent varieties. **Q.E.D.** 

	#### 6.11 Nonsingularity and projective varieties

Although the above results were discussed in terms of afine varieties, the idea of nonsingularity and of dimension applies directly to any variety V : a point $P \in V$ is nonsingular if it is a nonsingular point of an afine open $V _ { 0 } ~ \subset ~ V$ containing it; by Corollary 6.9, this notion does not depend on the choice of $V _ { 0 }$ . On the other hand, for a projective variety $V \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ , it is sometimes useful to consider the tangent space to $V$ at $P$ as a projective subspace of $\mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ . I give the definition for a hypersurface only: if $V = V ( f )$ is a hypersurface defined by a form (= homogeneous polynomial) $f \in k [ X _ { 0 } , \ldots , X _ { n } ]$ of degree $d ,$ and $V \ni P = \left( a _ { 0 } , \ldots , a _ { n } \right)$ , then $\sum { \partial f } / { \partial X _ { i } ( P ) } \cdot X _ { i } = 0$ is the equation of a hyperplane in $\mathbb P ^ { n }$ which plays the role of the tangent plane to $V$ at P . If $P \in \mathbb { A } _ { ( 0 ) } ^ { n } ,$ then this projective hyperplane is the projective closure of the afine tangent hyperplane to $V _ { ( 0 ) }$ at $P ,$ as can be checked easily using Euler’s formula: 

$$
\sum X _ {i} \cdot \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} = d f \quad \text { for } f \in k [ X _ {0}, \ldots , X _ {n} ] \text { homogeneous   of   degree } d.
$$

Because of this formula, to find out whether a point $P \in \mathbb { P } ^ { n }$ is a singular point of $V .$ , we only have to check $( n + 1 )$ out of the (n + 2) conditions 

$$
f (P) = 0, \quad \frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} (P) = 0 \mathrm{for} i = 0, \ldots , n,
$$

so that for example, if the degree of $f$ is not divisible by char $k ,$ 

$$
\frac {\partial f}{\partial X _ {i}} (P) = 0 \text {   for   } i = 0, \dots , n \implies f (P) = 0,
$$

and $P \in V$ is a singularity. 

	#### 6.12 Worked example: blowup

Let $B = \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 }$ with coordinates $( u , v )$ , and $\sigma \colon B \to \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 }$ the map $( u , v ) \mapsto ( x = u , y = u v ) \colon$ ; clearly, $\sigma$ is a birational morphism: it contracts the v-axis $\ell : ( u = 0 )$ to the origin 0 and is an isomorphism outside this exceptional set. Let’s find out what happens under σ to a curve $C : ( f = 0 ) \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 } \colon$ ; the question will only be of interest if $C$ passes through 0. 

Clearly $\sigma ^ { - 1 } ( C ) \subset B$ is the algebraic subset defined by $( f \circ \sigma ) ( u , v ) = f ( u , u v ) = 0 ;$ since $0 \in C$ by assumption, it follows that $\ell : ( u = 0 )$ is contained in $\sigma ^ { - 1 } ( C )$ , or equivalently, that u $\mid f ( u , u v )$ It’s easy to see that the highest power $u ^ { m }$ of u dividing $f ( u , u v )$ is equal to the smallest degree $m = a + b$ of the monomials $x ^ { a } y ^ { b }$ occurring in $f ,$ that is, the multiplicity of $f$ at 0; so $\sigma ^ { - 1 } ( C )$ decomposes as the union of the exceptional curve $\sigma ^ { - 1 } ( 0 ) = \ell$ (with multiplicity m), together with a new curve $C _ { 1 }$ defined by $f _ { 1 } ( u , v ) = f ( u , u v ) / u ^ { m }$ . Consider the examples 

- $f = \alpha x - y + \cdot \cdot \cdot ;$ 

- $f = y ^ { 2 } - x ^ { 2 } + \cdot \cdot \cdot ;$ 

$$
\text {(c)} f = y ^ {2} - x ^ {3},
$$

where . . . denotes terms of higher degree. Clearly in (a) f has multiplicity 1, and $f _ { 1 } = \alpha - v + \cdot \cdot \cdot$ (where · · · consists of terms divisible by u), so $C _ { 1 }$ is again nonsingular, and meets ` transversally at $( 0 , \alpha ) ;$ thus σ replaces $0 \in \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 }$ with the line ` whose points correspond to tangent directions at 0 (excluding $( x = 0 ) )$ . In (b) $f _ { 1 } = v ^ { 2 } - 1 + \cdot \cdot \cdot$ , so $C _ { 1 }$ has two nonsingular points $( 0 , \pm 1 )$ above $0 \in C$ ; thus the blowup $\sigma$ ‘separates the two branches’ of the singular curve C. In (c) $f _ { 1 } = v ^ { 2 } - u$ so that $C _ { 1 }$ is nonsingular, but above 0 it is tangent to the contracted curve `. 

In either case (b) or (c), σ replaces a singular curve C by a nonsingular one $C _ { 1 }$ birational to C (by introducing ‘new coordinates’ $u = x , v = y / x )$ This is what is meant by a resolution of singularities. In the case of plane curves, a resolution can always be obtained by a chain of blowups (see Ex. 6.6 for examples, and [Fulton, pp. 162–171] for more details), and the process of resolution gives detailed information about the singularities. A famous theorem of H. Hironaka guarantees the possibility of resolving singularities by blowups (in any dimension, over a field of characteristic zero). This is a crucial theoretical result that reduces the birational study of varieties to nonsingular ones; however, the actual process of resolution by blowups is in general extremely complicated, and does not necessarily contribute very much to the understanding of the singularities or varieties concerned.

I (V _ {f}) = (I (V), Y f - 1) \subset k [ X _ {1}, \dots , X _ {n}, Y ],

so that if $Q \ = \ ( a _ { 1 } , \ldots , a _ { n } , b ) \in V _ { f }$ , then $T _ { Q } V _ { f } \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n + 1 }$ is defined by the equations for $T _ { P } V \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { n }$ , together with one equation involving Y .]

$$
X _ {0} ^ {d} + X _ {1} ^ {d} + \dots + X _ {n} ^ {d} = 0
$$

is nonsingular (if char k does not divide $d )$ 

6.6 (a) Let $C _ { n } \subset \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 }$ be the curve given by $f _ { n } : y ^ { 2 } - x ^ { 2 n + 1 }$ and $\sigma \colon B \to \mathbb { A } ^ { 2 }$ be as in (6.12), with $\ell = \sigma ^ { - 1 } ( 0 )$ ; show that $\sigma ^ { - 1 } ( C _ { n } )$ decomposes as the union of ` together with a curve isomorphic to $C _ { n - 1 }$ . Deduce that $C _ { n }$ can be resolved by a chain of n blowups. 

- Show how to resolve the following curve singularities by making one or more blowups: 

1. $y ^ { 3 } = x ^ { 4 } ;$ ; 

2. $y ^ { 3 } = x ^ { 5 } ;$ 

3. $( y ^ { 2 } - x ^ { 2 } ) ( y ^ { 2 } - x ^ { 5 } ) = x ^ { 8 } .$

	### Chapter 7

	### The 27 lines on a cubic surface

In this section $S \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ will be a nonsingular cubic surface, given by a homogeneous cubic $f =$ $f ( X , Y , Z , T )$ . Consider the lines ` of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ lying on S. 

	#### 7.1 Consequences of nonsingularity

Proposition $( a )$ There exists at most 3 lines of S through any point $P \in S ;$ if there are 2 or $^ { 3 , }$ they must be coplanar. The picture is: 

![[9fbc0a7917413a0920b11730cd45dca11ca997be193c444a2285398c6b5c13d1.jpg]]

Figure 7.1: 3 concurrent lines or triangle

- Every plane $\Pi \subset \mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ intersects S in one of the following: 

1. an irreducible cubic; or 

2. a conic plus a line; or 

3. 3 distinct lines. 

**Proof.** (a) If $\ell \subset S$ then $\ell = T _ { P } \ell \subset T _ { P } S$ , so that all lines of S through P are contained in the plane $T _ { P } S ;$ there are at most 3 of them by (b). 

- I have to prove that a multiple line is impossible: if $\Pi : ( T = 0 )$ and $\ell : ( Z = 0 ) \subset \Pi$ , then to say that ` is a multiple line of S ∩ Π means that $f$ is of the form 

$$
f = Z ^ {2} \cdot A (X, Y, Z, T) + T \cdot B (X, Y, Z, T),
$$

with A a linear form, B a quadratic form. Then $S : ( f = 0 )$ is singular at a point where $Z = T =$ $B = 0 ;$ this is a nonempty set, since it is the set of roots of B on the line $\ell : ( Z = T = 0 )$ 

**Proposition 7.2** There exists at least one line ` on S. 

There are several approaches to proving this. A standard argument is by a dimension count: lines of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ are parametrised by a 4-dimensional variety, and for a line ` to lie on S imposes 4 conditions on ` (because the restriction of $f$ to ` is a cubic form, the 4 coeficients of which must vanish). A little work is needed to turn this into a rigorous proof, since a priori it shows only that the set of lines has dimension $\geq 0 .$ and not that it is nonempty (see the highbrow notes (8.15) for a discussion of the traditional Proof and the dificulties involved in it). 

It is also perfectly logical to assume the proposition (restrict attention only to cubic surfaces containing lines). I now explain how (7.2) can be proved by direct coordinate geometry and elimination. The Proof occupies the next 3 pages, and divides up into 4 steps; you can skip it if you prefer (GOTO 7.3). 

Step 1 (Preliminary construction) For any point $P \in S$ , the intersection of S with the tangent plane $T _ { P } S$ is a plane cubic $C = S \cap T _ { P } S .$ , which by Ex. 6.7 is singular at $P .$ I assume that C is irreducible, since otherwise P is on a line of S, and I’m home; then C is a nodal or cuspidal cubic, and the coordinate $( X , Y , Z , T )$ of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ can be chosen such that $T _ { P } S : ( T = 0 ) , P = ( 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 )$ , and 

$$
C: (X Y Z = X ^ {3} + Y ^ {3}) \text {   or   } (X ^ {2} Z = Y ^ {3}).
$$

Whether C is nodal or cuspidal for given $P \in S$ depends on the matrix of second derivatives (or Hessian matrix) of f at $P ;$ this is discussed in more detail in Ex. 7.3, which proves (in characteristic $\neq 2 )$ that the cuspidal case must occur for some point $P \in S$ . For simplicity, I prove (7.2) in the cuspidal case; in principle, the Proof goes through in exactly the same way in the nodal case, but the elimination calculation gets much nastier (see Ex. 7.10). Thus assume that 

$$
f = X ^ {2} Z - Y ^ {3} + g T,
$$

where $g = g _ { 2 } ( X , Y , Z , T )$ is a quadratic form; $g ( 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 ) \neq 0$ by nonsingularity of S at $P ,$ so I can assume that $g ( 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 ) = 1$ 

Step 2 (Statement of main claim) Consider the variable point $P _ { \alpha } = ( 1 , \alpha , \alpha ^ { 3 } , 0 )$ of $C \subset S$ Any line of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ through $P _ { \alpha }$ meets the complementary plane $\boldsymbol { \Pi } : ( \boldsymbol { X } = 0 )$ in a point $Q = ( 0 , Y , Z , T )$ . I write out the equations for the line $P _ { \alpha } Q$ to be contained in S in terms of α and $Q ;$ expanding $f ( \lambda P _ { \alpha } + \mu Q )$ in powers of λ and µ gives 

$$
P _ {\alpha} Q \subset S \iff A (Y, Z, T) = B (Y, Z, T) = C (Y, Z, T) = 0,
$$

where A, B and C are forms of degree 1, 2 and 3 in $( Y , Z , T )$ , whose coeficients involve α. 

Main Claim There exists a ‘resultant’ polynomial $R _ { 2 7 } ( \alpha )$ , which is monic of degree 27 in α, such that 

$$
R (\alpha) = 0 \iff A = B = C = 0 \text {   have   a   common   zero   } (\eta : \zeta : \tau) \text {   in   } \mathbb {P} ^ {2}.
$$

This statement proves (7.2), since it implies that for every root α of $R ,$ there exists a point $Q = ( 0 : \eta : \zeta : \tau )$ in Π for which the line $P _ { \alpha } Q$ is contained in S. The idea here is a standard elimination calculation based on Ex. 1.10; the rest of the Proof is concerned with writing out A, B and C explicitly to prove the claim. 

Step 3 (Polar form) Define the polar of $f$ to be the form in two sets of variables $( X , Y , Z , T )$ and $( X ^ { \prime } , \dot { Y ^ { \prime } } , Z ^ { \prime } , T ^ { \prime } )$ given by 

$$
f _ {1} (X, Y, Z, T; X ^ {\prime}, Y ^ {\prime}, Z ^ {\prime}, T ^ {\prime}) = \frac {\partial f}{\partial X} \cdot X ^ {\prime} + \frac {\partial f}{\partial Y} \cdot Y ^ {\prime} + \frac {\partial f}{\partial Z} \cdot Z ^ {\prime} + \frac {\partial f}{\partial T} \cdot T ^ {\prime}.
$$

It’s clear from the definition of tangent space (see (6.4) and (6.10)) that for $P = ( X , Y , Z , T ) \in S$ and $P \neq Q = ( X ^ { \prime } , Y ^ { \prime } , Z ^ { \prime } , T ^ { \prime } ) \in \mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ 

$f _ { 1 } ( P ; Q ) = 0 \iff$ the line P Q is tangent to $S$ at $P .$ 

Clearly 

$$
f (\lambda P + \mu Q) = \lambda^ {3} f (P) + \lambda^ {2} \mu f _ {1} (P; Q) + \lambda \mu^ {2} f _ {1} (Q; P) + \mu^ {3} f (Q),
$$

so that for $P \neq Q \in \mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ , the 4 conditions 

$$
f (P) = f _ {1} (P; Q) = f _ {1} (Q; P) = f (Q)
$$

are the equations for the line $\ell = P Q$ to be contained in $S : ( f = 0 )$ . More geometrically, these say that ` is tangent to $S$ at both P and $Q ,$ , so that $f | \ell$ has double roots at both points, and then $\ell \subset S$ follows from Proposition 1.8. 

The polar of $f = X ^ { 2 } Z - Y ^ { 3 } + g T$ is 

$$
f _ {1} = 2 X Z \cdot X ^ {\prime} - 3 Y ^ {2} \cdot Y ^ {\prime} + X ^ {2} \cdot Z ^ {\prime} + g (X, Y, Z, T) \cdot T ^ {\prime} + T g _ {1}.
$$

Here $g _ { 1 } = g _ { 1 } ( X , Y , Z , T ; X ^ { \prime } , Y ^ { \prime } , Z ^ { \prime } , T ^ { \prime } )$ is the polar form of $g$ defined in the same way as above; since $g$ is quadratic, g<sub>1</sub> is a symmetric bilinear form such that $g _ { 1 } ( P , P ) = 2 g ( P )$ 

Substituting $P _ { \alpha } = ( 1 , \alpha , \alpha ^ { 3 } , 0 )$ and $Q = ( 0 , Y , Z , T )$ gives the equations for $P _ { \alpha } Q \subset S$ as $A =$ $B = C = 0$ , where 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & {A = Z - 3 \alpha^ {2} Y + g (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0) T,} \\ & {B = - 3 \alpha Y ^ {2} + g _ {1} (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0; 0, Y, Z, T) T,} \\ & {C = - Y ^ {3} + g (0, Y, Z, T) T.} \end{array}
$$

Step 4 (Elimination calculation) I now eliminate $Y , Z , T$ from the above 3 equations, paying attention to the highest powers of α occurring. Note that since $g ( 0 , 0 , 1 , 0 ) = 1$ , it follows that 

$$
g (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0) = \alpha^ {6} + \dots = a ^ {(6)},
$$

where $\cdot \cdot \cdot$ denotes terms of lower degree in $\alpha ;$ thus $a ^ { ( 6 ) }$ is monic of degree 6. Then $A = 0$ gives $Z$ as a linear form in $Y$ and $T _ { \cdot }$ , 

$$
Z = 3 \alpha^ {2} Y - a ^ {(6)} T.
$$

Substituting in B, and using the bilinearity of $g _ { 1 }$ gives 

$$
\begin{array}{r l} & B = - 3 \alpha Y ^ {2} + g _ {1} (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0; 0, Y, 3 \alpha^ {2} Y - a ^ {(6)} T, T) T \\ & \quad = b _ {0} Y ^ {2} + b _ {1} Y T + b _ {2} T ^ {2}, \end{array}
$$

where 

$$
b _ {0} = - 3 \alpha , b _ {1} = g _ {1} (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0; 0, 1, 3 \alpha^ {2}, 0) = 6 \alpha^ {5} + \dots ,
$$

$$
b _ {2} = g _ {1} (1, \alpha , \alpha^ {3}, 0; 0, 0, - a (6), 1) = - 2 \alpha^ {9} + \dots .
$$

Similarly, substituting for $Z$ in $C ,$ and expanding the quadratic form g gives 

$$
C = - Y ^ {3} + g (0, Y, 3 \alpha^ {2} Y - a ^ {(6)} T, T) T = c _ {0} Y ^ {3} + c _ {1} Y ^ {2} T + c _ {2} Y T ^ {2} + c _ {3} T ^ {3},
$$

where 

$$
\begin{array}{l} c _ {0} = - 1, \quad c _ {1} = g (0, 1, 3 \alpha^ {2}, 0) = 9 \alpha^ {4} + \dots , \\ c _ {2} = g _ {1} (0, 1, 3 \alpha^ {2}, 0; 0, 0, - a (6), 1) = - 6 \alpha^ {8} + \dots , \\ c _ {3} = g (0, 0, - a (6), 1) = \alpha^ {1 2} + \dots . \end{array}
$$

Now by the result of Ex. 1.10, $B ^ { \prime }$ and $C ^ { \prime }$ have a common zero $( \eta : \tau )$ if and only if 

$$
\det \left| \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} & & \\ & - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} & \\ & & - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} \\ - 1 & 9 \alpha^ {4} & - 6 \alpha^ {8} & \alpha^ {1 2} & \\ & - 1 & 9 \alpha^ {4} & - 6 \alpha^ {8} & \alpha^ {1 2} \end{array} \right| = 0.
$$

The determinant is a polynomial in $\alpha ,$ and it’s not hard to see that its leading term comes from taking the leading term in each entry of the determinant: 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \det \left| \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} & & \\ & - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} & \\ & & - 3 \alpha & 6 \alpha^ {5} & - 2 \alpha^ {9} \\ - 1 & 9 \alpha^ {4} & - 6 \alpha^ {8} & \alpha^ {1 2} & \\ & - 1 & 9 \alpha^ {4} & - 6 \alpha^ {8} & \alpha^ {1 2} \end{array} \right| = \alpha^ {2 7} \cdot \det \left| \begin{array}{c c c c c} - 3 & 6 & 2 & & \\ & - 3 & 6 & 2 & \\ & & - 3 & 6 & 2 \\ - 1 & 9 & - 6 & 1 & \\ & - 1 & 9 & - 6 & 1 \end{array} \right| \\ = \alpha^ {2 7}. \end{array}
$$

This completes the Proof of the main claim. **Q.E.D.** 

**Proposition 7.3** Given a line $\ell \subset S$ , there exist exactly 5 pairs $( \ell _ { i } , \ell _ { i } ^ { \prime } )$ of lines of $S$ meeting $\ell ,$ in such a way that

**Proof.** (taken from [Beauville, $p . ~ 5 { 1 } \jmath )$ If Π is a plane of $\mathbb { P } ^ { 3 }$ containing ` then $\Pi \cap S = \ell +$ conic (since $f | \Pi$ is divisible by the equation of `). This conic can either be singular or nonsingular:

I have to prove that there are exactly 5 distinct planes $\Pi _ { i } \supset \ell$ for which the singular case occurs. The fact stated as property (ii) that lines in diferent planes are disjoint will then follow from (7.1, a). 

Suppose that $\ell : ( Z = T = 0 )$ ; then I can expand f out as 

$$
f = A X ^ {2} + B X Y + C Y ^ {2} + D X + E Y + F,\tag{\( (*) \}
$$

where $A , B , C , D , E , F \in k [ Z , T ]$ , with A, B and C linear forms, D and E quadratic forms, and F a cubic form. If I consider this equation as a variable conic in X and $Y$ , it is singular if and only if 

$$
\Delta (Z, T) = \det \left| \begin{array}{c c c} A & B & D \\ B & C & E \\ D & E & F \end{array} \right| = 4 A C F + B D E - A E ^ {2} - B ^ {2} F - C D ^ {2} = 0.
$$

(Here $\Delta$ is 4 times the usual determinant if char $\neq 2 ;$ in characteristic 2 the statement is an easy exercise.) 

To be more precise, any plane through ` is given by $\Pi : ( \mu Z = \lambda T ) ; { \mathrm { i f } } \mu \neq 0$ , I can assume $\mu = 1$ so that $Z = \lambda T$ . Then in terms of the homogeneous coordinates $( X , Y , T )$ on Π, $f | \Pi = T { \cdot } Q ( X , Y , T )$ where 

$$
\begin{array}{c} Q = A (\lambda , 1) X ^ {2} + B (\lambda , 1) X Y + C (\lambda , 1) Y ^ {2} \\ \qquad + D (\lambda , 1) T X + E (\lambda , 1) T Y + F (\lambda , 1) T ^ {2}. \end{array}
$$

Now $\Delta ( Z , T )$ is a homogeneous quintic, so by (1.8), it has 5 roots counted with multiplicities. To prove the proposition, I have to show that it doesn’t have multiple roots; this also is a consequence of the nonsingularity of S. 

Claim $\Delta ( Z , T )$ has only simple roots. 

Suppose $Z = 0$ is a root of $\Delta .$ and let $\Pi : ( Z = 0 )$ be the corresponding plane; I have to prove that $\Delta$ is not divisible by $Z ^ { 2 }$ . By the above picture, $\Pi \cap S$ is a set of 3 lines, and according to whether they are concurrent, I can arrange the coordinates so that 

either (i) $\ell : ( T = 0 ) , \ell _ { 1 } : ( X = 0 ) , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } : ( Y = 0 ) .$ 2 

or (ii) $\ell : ( T = 0 ) , \ell _ { 1 } : ( X = 0 ) , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } : ( X = T ) .$ 

Hence, in case $( \mathrm { i } ) , f = X Y T + Z g$ , with g quadratic, and in terms of the expression (∗), this means that $B = T + a Z$ , and $Z \mid A , C , D , E , F$ . Therefore, modulo terms divisible by $Z ^ { 2 }$ 

$$
\Delta \equiv - T ^ {2} F \mod Z ^ {2}.
$$

In addition, the point $P = ( 0 , 0 , 0 , 1 ) \in S$ , and nonsingularity at $P$ means that F must contain the term $Z T ^ { 2 }$ with nonzero coeficient. In particular, $Z ^ { 2 }$ does not divide F . Therefore $( Z = 0 )$ is a simple root of $\Delta$ . 

Case (ii) is a similar calculation (see Ex. 7.1).

	#### 7.7 The configuration of lines

An alternative statement is that the lines of S are $\ell , \ell _ { 1 } , \dots , \ell _ { 5 } , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } , \dots , \ell _ { 5 } ^ { \prime }$ , and 16 other lines which meet an odd number of $\ell _ { 1 } , \ldots , \ell _ { 5 } \colon$ 

$$
\begin{array}{c} \ell_ {i} ^ {\prime \prime} \text {meets} \ell_ {i} \text {only} \\ \ell_ {i j k} \text {meets} \ell_ {i}, \ell_ {j}, \ell_ {k} \text {only} \\ m \text {meets all of} \ell_ {1}, \ldots , \ell_ {5}. \end{array}
$$

In the notation I have introduced, it is easy to see that the incidence relation between the $2 7$ lines of S is as follows: 

` meets $\ell _ { 1 } , \ldots , \ell _ { 5 } , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } , \ldots , \ell _ { 5 } ^ { \prime } ;$ ; 

$\ell _ { 1 }$ meets $\ell , m , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime } , \ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime \prime }$ , and $\ell _ { 1 j k }$ for 6 choices of $\{ j , k \} \subset \{ 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 \}$ ; 

$\ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime }$ meets $\ell , \ell _ { 1 } , \ell _ { j } ^ { \prime \prime }$ (for 4 choices of $j \neq 1 )$ , and $\ell _ { i j k }$ (for 4 choices of $\{ i , j , k \} \subset \{ 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 \} )$ ; 

$\ell _ { 1 } ^ { \prime \prime }$ meets $m , \ell _ { 1 } , \ell _ { j } ^ { \prime }$ (for 4 choices of $j \neq 1 )$ , and $\ell _ { i j k }$ (for 4 choices of $\{ i , j , k \} \subset \{ 2 , 3 , 4 , 5 \} )$ ; 

$\ell _ { 1 2 3 }$ meets $\ell _ { 1 } , \ell _ { 2 } , \ell _ { 3 } , \ell _ { 1 4 5 } , \ell _ { 2 4 5 } , \ell _ { 3 4 5 } , \ell _ { 4 } ^ { \prime } , \ell _ { 5 } ^ { \prime } , \ell _ { 4 } ^ { \prime \prime } , \ell _ { 5 } ^ { \prime \prime } .$
